Division of Oncology 2/Office of Oncologic Diseases

Mirdametinib
GOMEKLI
Kinase inhibitor
PD-0325901
SpringWorks Therapeutics
Tablets and capsules
2 mg/m2 orally twice daily, with or without food, for the first 21
days of each 28-day cycle. Continue treatment with GOMEKLI
until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity.
Applicant Proposed For the treatment of adult and pediatric patients 2 years of age
Indication(s)/Population(s) and older with neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1) who have
symptomatic plexiform neurofibromas (PN).
Recommendation on Approval
Regulatory Action
Recommended For the treatment of adult and pediatric patients 2 years of age
Indication(s)/Population(s) and older with neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1) who have
(if applicable) symptomatic plexiform neurofibromas (PN) not amenable to
complete resection.

NDA/IND Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 219379/219389
Mirdametinib (GOMEKLI)

1 Executive Summary
Product Introduction
Mirdametinib is a small molecule inhibitor of mitogen-activated protein kinases 1 and 2
(MEK1/2). MEK1/2 proteins are upstream regulators of the extracellular signal-related kinase
(ERK) pathway. Activation of this signaling pathway leads to cell proliferation and is involved in
various types of cancer (Yaeger and Corcoran, 2019).
Neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1) is an autosomal dominant disorder with variable expressivity
caused by germline mutations in the NF1 gene. Plexiform neurofibromas (PNs) are benign
tumors of the peripheral nerve sheath which occur in 40-ϱϬй ŽĨ ƉĂƚŝĞŶƚƐ ǁŝƚŚ E&ϭ͘ PNs may be
associated with significant morbidity including pain, disfigurement, neurologic deficits, and
functional impairment; and mortality from malignant transformation to malignant peripheral
nerve sheath tumors (MPNST) and compression of vital structures (Ly and Blakeley, 2019).
In a mouse model of neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1), oral dosing of mirdametinib inhibited ERK
phosphorylation and reduced neurofibroma tumor volume and proliferation.
In this new drug application (NDA), the Applicant proposed the following indication for
mirdametinib:
x Adult and pediatric patients 2 years of age and older with neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1)
who have symptomatic plexiform neurofibromas.
The review team recommends approval for adult and pediatric patients 2 years of age and older
with neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1) who have symptomatic plexiform neurofibromas (PN) not
amenable to complete resection.
The recommended dosage of mirdametinib is 2 mg/m2 orally twice daily with or without food
for the first 21 days of each 28-day cycle, until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity.

Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
The review team recommends regular approval of mirdametinib for the treatment of adult and
pediatric patients 2 years of age and older with neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1) who have
symptomatic plexiform neurofibromas not amenable to complete surgical resection. The
recommendation for approval is primarily supported by overall response rate (ORR) and
duration of response (DOR) results from Study MEK-NF-201 (which will be referred to as the
ReNeu study in the review). In this study, mirdametinib demonstrated a robust and clinically
meaningful effect on ORR that is durable and associated with improvements in disease-related
morbidities. These factors represent direct clinical benefit to patients and support regular
16

Mirdametinib (GOMEKLI)
approval. The safety profile for mirdametinib is consistent with other MEK inhibitors and is
acceptable in the context of a serious and life-threatening disease.
The ReNeu study is a multicenter, single-arm trial investigating mirdametinib in adult and
pediatric patients with NF1 and progressive or symptomatic plexiform neurofibromas not
amenable to complete surgical resection. Eligible patients were at least 2 years of age with PN
causing significant morbidity, and a target PN that was evaluable by volumetric analysis.
Patients were enrolled by age group into pediatric and adult cohorts. Treatment consisted of
mirdametinib at a dosage of 2 mg/m2 orally twice daily for the first 21 days of each 28-day cycle.
The study included a treatment phase lasting 24 cycles and an optional long-term follow-up
phase. The primary endpoint to demonstrate the efficacy of mirdametinib for this population
was confirmed ORR͕ ĚĞĨŝŶĞĚ ĂƐ WE ĚĞĐƌĞĂƐĞ шϮϬй ĐŽŵƉĂƌĞĚ ƚŽ ďĂƐĞůŝŶĞ ŝŶ ĐŽŶƐĞĐƵƚŝǀĞ ƐĐĂŶƐ
within 2-6 months, using independent central review of MRI volumetric analysis. DOR was a key
secondary endpoint.
The primary efficacy population consisted of 114 patients, including 56 pediatric and 58 adult
patients. The confirmed BICR-assessed ORR in pediatric patients was ϱϮй ;ϵϱй /͗ 38, 65); the

Supportive data for the application were derived from the observed ORR in 19 patients treated
with mirdametinib on Study NF-106 [32й ;ϵϱй /͗ ϭ3, 57], a single arm, multicenter trial of
adolescent and adult patients with an unresectable PN with progression within the past year.
In addition to radiographic tumor shrinkage, the ReNeu study evaluated mirdametinib effects
on PN-related functional impairment, symptoms and disfigurement using various clinical
outcome assessment (COA) measures as well as longitudinal photographic evaluations. Despite
the limitations of the single-arm trial design, small sample size, heterogeneity of associated
morbidities and interpretation of the COA measures, a general trend toward an association
between a reduction in tumor volume and durable improvement in clinical outcomes was
observed.
Given the benign, albeit morbid, nature of NF1-PN, the Applicant submitted reports from the
literature indicating that a key characteristic of NF1 PN is the uncommon occurrence of
spontaneous regression, supporting the conclusion that the observed tumor responses in
ReNeu are due to the effect of the drug. This supportive information includes reports from the
placebo control arm of the National Cancer Institute (NCI) Study 01-C-0222, a randomized,
placebo-controlled trial of tipifarnib in pediatric patients with NF1 and progressive PNs; and NCI
Study 08-C-0079, a natural history study of children, adolescents, and adults with NF1.

NDA/IND Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 219379/219389
Mirdametinib (GOMEKLI)
FDA considers ORR, supported by DOR, to be an appropriate endpoint for regular approval for
this rare patient population given that their clinical meaningfulness is associated with improved
clinical outcome assessment data from the ReNeu study. Furthermore, while rare instances of
spontaneous PN volume decrease have been observed, the magnitude of these reductions are
not comparable to the tumor regression observed with mirdametinib treatment. There are no
approved therapies for adults with NF1-PN. Selumetinib is a MEK inhibitor that is approved for
pediatric patients with symptomatic, inoperable NF1-PN; however, the approval of
mirdametinib addresses an unmet need for pediatric patients with NF1-PN who are unable to
swallow capsules.
The observed ORR and DOR with mirdametinib are robust and clinically meaningful and the
tumor shrinkage results are associated with a positive trend in improved clinical outcomes in
patients with symptomatic NF1-PN not amenable to surgical resection, and provide substantial
evidence of effectiveness. The safety findings support a favorable benefit:risk assessment, and
the review team recommends regular approval.

NDA/IND Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 219379/219389
Mirdametinib (GOMEKLI)

2 Therapeutic Context
Analysis of Condition
The Applicant’s Position:
Neurofibromatosis is classified into three major clinically and genetically distinct forms:
neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1), neurofibromatosis type 2, and schwannomatosis. NF1, also
known as von Recklinghausen disease, is the most common form of neurofibromatosis. NF1 is a
rare, autosomal dominant genetic disorder with an annual birth incidence of approximately 1 in
2500 individuals (Children’s Tumor Foundation 2023; Lee 2023). Approximately one-half of
cases are inherited with the remainder being the result of de novo mutations in the NF1 tumor
suppressor gene, located at chromosome 17q11.2 (Evans 2010; Ledbetter 1989; Feldkamp
1998).
Neurofibromin, the protein product encoded by the NF1 gene, belongs to a family of guanosine
triphosphate hydrolase (GTPase)-activating proteins that down-regulate the cellular protooncogene p21-ras (21 kD rat sarcoma viral oncogene homologs) (Martin 1990; Weiss 1999;
Gutmann 2013). Ras activates numerous signaling pathways, including the mitogen-activated
protein kinase (MAPK) pathways. Ras has been implicated in the control of cell growth and
differentiation, and the ability of neurofibromin to down-regulate p21-ras suggests that the loss
of neurofibromin may lead to uncontrolled cellular growth or tumor formation.
Mutations in the NF1 gene result in loss of production or reduced function of neurofibromin,
causing the wide spectrum of clinical findings, including NF1-associated tumors (Shen 1996).
Penetrance, or the likelihood that the individual carrying the mutation will manifest the
disorder, is complete. The loss of neurofibromin in malignancy supports the notion that
neurofibromin is a tumor-suppressor protein (Bergoug 2020).
NF1 is characterized by diverse, progressive cutaneous, neurological, skeletal, and neoplastic
manifestations. The manifestation of the NF1 clinical diagnostic criteria typically appear in the
order of café-au-lait macules, axillary and/or inguinal freckling, Lisch nodules (iris hamartomas),
and finally neurofibromas (DeBella 2000). Most importantly, patients with NF1 develop both
benign and malignant tumors at increased frequency throughout life (Gutmann 1997;
Seminog 2013). The diagnostic criteria for NF1 were defined at a National Institute of Health
(NIH) consensus development conference in 1987 (NIH Consensus Development Conference
Statement 1987) and updated in 2021. In most cases, the diagnosis of NF1 is made on clinical
grounds, requiring 2 or more clinical features to be present from the defined list of diagnostic
criteria for NF1 presented below:
x

Six or more café-au-lait-macules*

Neurofibromas are benign peripheral nerve sheath tumors that are comprised of a mixture of
Schwann cells, fibroblasts, perineural cells, and mast cells (Tucker 2011). When a neurofibroma
extends longitudinally along a nerve and involves multiple fascicles, it is classified as a plexiform
neurofibroma (PN). PNs grow most rapidly during the first decade of life and while the growth
rate is highly variable between patients, the growth rate of PN in younger children is generally
much greater compared to older children or adults (Dombi 2007, Nguyen 2012, Tucker 2009). A
Natural History study of NF1 conducted by the National Cancer Institute (NCI) Pediatric
Oncology Branch demonstrated that, although spontaneous PN shrinkage occurred in a small
number of PNs in older adolescent and adult patients, the shrinkage is very slow, taking place
over many years, and no patient had spontaneous PN shrinkage шϮϬй ǁŝƚŚŝŶ Ă ǇĞĂƌ
(Dombi 2007, Gross 2018). As such, PNs rarely regress spontaneously, and in many patients
ƚŚĞŝƌ ŐƌŽǁƚŚ ŝƐ ƌĞůĞŶƚůĞƐƐ͘ WEƐ ŽĐĐƵƌ ŝŶ ϯϬй-ϱϬй ŽĨ ƉĂƚŝĞŶƚƐ ǁŝƚŚ E&ϭ ĂŶĚ ƌĞƉƌĞƐĞŶƚ Ă ŵĂũŽƌ
cause of morbidity and disfigurement in individuals with NF1, and when symptomatic, are
associated with increased mortality (Prada 2012; Miller 2019). As tumor growth progresses, PNs
produce dysfunction, pain, and cosmetic disfigurement and can compress vital structures such
as the airway or spinal cord. Once a clinical morbidity develops in patients with PN that are
growing, it is extremely unlikely for the morbidity to resolve spontaneously (Gross 2018).
Chronic pain in NF1 can be associated with PN, as well as subcutaneous neurofibroma and bony
deformations (scoliosis and pseudoarthrosis), and as such, can occur anywhere in the body or
multiple sites at the same time (Bellampalli 2019). Furthermore, PNs have the potential to
undergo malignant transformation into malignant peripheral nerve sheath tumors (MPNSTs),
ǁŚŝĐŚ ŽĐĐƵƌ ŝŶ ĂŶ ĞƐƚŝŵĂƚĞĚ ϴй-ϭϲй ŽĨ ƉĂƚŝĞŶƚƐ ǁŝƚŚ E&ϭ ;Evans 2002; Hirbe 2014). MPNSTs
are aggressive tumors that can metastasize, with a 5-ǇĞĂƌ ƐƵƌǀŝǀĂů ƌĂƚĞ ŽĨ ϮϬй-ϯϬй

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of the condition. FDA also notes that during the
NF1 Natural History study conducted at the NCI, spontaneous gradual tumor volume was
confirmed in 10/113 (ϴ͘ϴйͿ ŽĨ WEƐ, with a median decrease from maximum volume of ϭϵй ĂŶĚ
a median decrease per year of 3.ϲй (Akshintala et al, 2020).
On June 13, 2019, FDA hosted a listening session for patients with NF, which included those
affected by PN, and their caregivers. During this session, patients described the symptoms, daily
burden, and impact of living with NF. Common themes in their narratives included the physical
burden of chronic pain, multiple surgeries, motor dysfunction, side effects of chemotherapy,
and the social and emotional toll of disfigurement. For example, one 19-year-old male with NF1
and multiple PNs, including a tumor which compressed his spinal cord, described undergoing
over twenty surgeries and receiving chemotherapy for 17 years to control PN growth. He also
described bullying by classmates that required him to change schools.

Analysis of Current Treatment Options
The Applicant’s Position:
The approach to treatment of the various tumors associated with NF1 depends upon the type
of tumor, its effect on adjacent tissues, and related complications. PNs usually involve multiple
nerve fascicles, with serpiginous growth and significant vascularity. These lesions can represent
a significant challenge in surgical treatment and pain management, especially with progressive
growth along the spinal column that may result in compression of the spinal cord (Gold 2013).
The management of these complex lesions varies. Patients were more likely to benefit from
surgery when the indications were airway compression or disfigurement (Canavese 2011).
,ŽǁĞǀĞƌ͕ ƵƉ ƚŽ ϰϰй ŽĨ ƚƵŵŽƌƐ ƉƌŽŐƌĞƐƐ ĂĨƚĞƌ ƚŚĞ ĨŝƌƐƚ ƐƵƌŐĞƌǇ͕ ŵŽƐƚ ĐŽŵŵŽŶůǇ ŝŶ ƉĂƚŝĞŶƚƐ
younger than 10 years of age, with head and neck tumors that could not be completely
resected (Needle 1997). Surgical resection is often limited to debulking of a specific area of a
large lesion, for example, when a component is impinging on the spinal cord or airway, or a
large soft tissue component is removed to improve cosmesis. Investigational studies of various
tyrosine kinase inhibitors, mTOR inhibitors, a farnesyl transferase inhibitor, and fibroblast
inhibitors have not demonstrated meaningful or durable objective responses in patients with
progressive PN (Jakacki 2017, Robertson 2012, Weiss 2014, Widemann 2014,
Widemann 2014b). One treatment option, selumetinib (Koselugo®), an oral selective MAPK
inhibitor, is currently approved in the United States (US) for the treatment of pediatric patients

does not have an age-appropriate formulation and cannot be administered to patients who
have difficulty swallowing a capsule whole.

FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position, and that there is an unmet medical need for adults
with symptomatic, inoperable NF1 PN as well as pediatric patients who are unable to swallow
capsules.

3 Regulatory Background

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s timeline, with the following additional comments regarding the
discussion at selected meetings:
x Pre-IND meeting August 29, 2018
o FDA stated that the Applicant would need to conduct a full battery of GLPcompliant reproductive toxicology studies and carcinogenicity studies and
recommended that the Applicant submit carcinogenicity protocols for special
protocol assessment review by FDA prior to initiation of studies (subsequently
reviewed by FDA on June 25, 2024). The Applicant confirmed that an embryofetal study was conducted in the rabbit, however FDA noted that the data from
the EFD study were not submitted. FDA recommended that the Applicant submit
the results of the study and ask for FDA feedback regarding whether pre and
post-natal development and pivotal EFD studies are necessary.
o FDA stated that the single-arm MEK-NF-201 study, with a primary endpoint of
radiographic response, might support an application seeking marketing approval,
provided that the natural history of the disease has been adequately established.
FDA also stated that the study should demonstrate a clinically meaningful
magnitude of response rate, that response rates are sufficiently durable, and
unequivocal evidence of an important treatment effect that is supported by data
showing an improvement in physical function or an improvement in specific
symptoms related to progressive PN disease that correlates with radiographic
tumor shrinkage.
x Type C meeting Jan 29, 2019
o FDA agreed with the Applicant’s plan to analyze age-based cohorts
independently given the age-dependent differences in tumor growth rate.
o FDA stated that the primary analysis for ORR should be based on all treated
patients who received at least one dose of mirdametinib; the Applicant indicated
agreement.
o FDA requested that the Applicant provide detailed plans for the analysis of
Clinical Outcome Assessment Data; the Applicant agreed to provide this
information in the Statistical Analysis plan prior to the start of study enrollment.
x Type C meeting October 13, 2021
o FDA strongly recommended that the Applicant request a meeting to discuss the
overall clinical development program for mirdametinib, specifically regarding the
approach to dosage optimization and the clinical outcome assessments and
patient reported outcomes being used to support the primary endpoint of Study
MEK-NF-201.

Mirdametinib (GOMEKLI)

5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
Executive Summary
Mirdametinib is an orally bioavailable small molecule non-ATP competitive inhibitor of
mitogen-activated protein kinase (MAPK)/extracellular signal regulated kinases 1 and 2
(MEK1/2). MEK1/2 proteins are upstream regulators of the extracellular signal-related kinase
(ERK) pathway. Both MEK and ERK are critical components of the RAS-regulated RAF-MEK-ERK
pathway, which is often activated in different types of cancers. The proposed indication for
mirdametinib is the treatment of adult and pediatric patients with neurofibromatosis type 1
(NF1)-related tumors. Neurofibromin (encoded by NF1) contains a RAS-GTPase activating
domain. Mutations in the NF1 gene result in loss of production or reduced function of
neurofibromin, causing hyperactive Ras signaling, that results in a wide spectrum of clinical
findings, including benign or malignant NF1-associated tumors.
The established pharmacological class of mirdametinib is kinase inhibitor. In biochemical
assays, mirdametinib inhibited the kinase activity of MEK1 and MEK2 with inhibitory constant
(Ki) values of 1.1 nM and 0.79 nM, respectively. In a cell-free signaling cascade assay,
mirdametinib inhibition of MEK reduced the level of phosphorylated ERK1 (pERK1) with a Ki
value of 0.9 nM. Mirdametinib and its pharmacologically active unique human metabolite, M22,
reduced the levels of pERK in cells in vitro, but M22 demonstrated considerably lower ability to
reduce the levels of pERK (>91-times lower). In addition, the carboxylic acid metabolite of
mirdametinib, PD-0315209, demonstrated inhibitory activity against MEK1 phosphorylation in a
cell-based assay (IC50=1.3 μM) but was less active compared to mirdametinib (IC50=0.59 nM).
The Applicant further demonstrated the antitumor activity of mirdametinib in a transgenic
mouse model of NF1 neurofibroma (Nf1fl/fl;Dhh-Cre ). The Applicant cites literature [Jessen
(2013), Jousma (2015)] to support the claim that in this model, deletion of Nf1 gene in Schwann
cells exhibits similar neurofibroma growth and histology to human neurofibromas. In this
model, spontaneous neurofibromas are detected by magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) at
approximately 4 months of age. Daily treatment of animals with mirdametinib for two months
(from 7 to 9 months of age) resulted in reduced tumor growth compared to untreated animals.
Protein lysates of tumor tissue harvested from mice treated with mirdametinib showed
reduced levels of pERK, a downstream target of MEK signaling. The results of the pharmacology
studies support the proposed mechanism of action of mirdametinib and proof-of-concept for
the proposed indication.

Mirdametinib was shown to absorb light in the 290 to 700 nm range, indicating probable
phototoxicity.

Though genetic toxicity of the PD-0315209 metabolite was not directly tested, it is expected
that rats were exposed to PD-0315209 in the in vivo genetic toxicology tests as rats can
generate PD-0315209. As discussed above, because M22 is a Phase 2 glucuronide conjugate, it
is not expected to have an increased risk of carcinogenicity and further evaluation is not
warranted.
Plexiform neurofibromas with NF-1 are generally slow growing and depending on location are
not immediately life-threatening but can be debilitating. Because of the potential for prolonged
treatment and long-term survival in the proposed patient population, the Applicant conducted
a carcinogenicity study and a dedicated fertility and early embryonic development study. In a 6month carcinogenicity study in CByB6F1/Tg rasH2 hemizygous mice, the FDA concluded that no
carcinogenic effect related to mirdametinib administration was observed at any dose level
tested (up to 5 mg/kg/day). The Applicant is currently conducting a 2-year carcinogenicity study
in rats and a post-marketing requirement (PMR) will be issued for the submission of the final
study report.
The effects of mirdametinib on fertility were assessed in a dedicated fertility study, where male
rats were treated with mirdametinib at doses of 0.1, 0.3, or 1 mg/kg/day for 28 days before
mating with untreated females to Gestational Day 1. In a separate cohort, female rats were
treated with mirdametinib at doses of 0.1, 0.3, or 1 mg/kg/day for 14 days before mating with
untreated males to Gestational Day 7. Though mirdametinib did not affect fertility in this study,
findings from the repeat-dose toxicology studies indicate that mirdametinib may impair female

Based on the mechanism of action of mirdametinib and findings in animals, the
pharmacology/toxicology team recommends a warning for embryo-fetal toxicity in the label for
GOMEKLI. Embryo-fetal developmental (EFD) studies were conducted as dose range finding
studies in rats and rabbits. Toxicokinetic evaluations were not included in the rat study; thus,
for consistency, animal to human dose comparisons were made based on body surface area
(BSA). Mirdametinib showed signs of maternal toxicity in rats at 5 mg/kg (30 mg/m2), including
decreases in body weight gain and food consumption, as well as scabs, sores, and abrasions in

shortening of limbs and absence or shortening of digits, were observed in one fetus and

the FDA agreed that no further reproductive toxicology testing (i.e., GLP embryo-fetal
development and pre-and post-natal development studies) was warranted.
FDA recommends advising females of reproductive potential to use effective contraception
during treatment with GOMEKLI and for 6 weeks after the last dose and male patients with
female partners of reproductive potential to use effective contraception during treatment with
GOMEKLI for 3 months after the last dose. The recommendation is consistent with drugs that
are teratogenic or embryo-fetal lethal and aneugenic and takes into consideration the half-life
of 28 hours for mirdametinib. Because of the potential for serious adverse effects of GOMEKLI
in a breastfed child, the review team recommends advising patients not to breastfeed during
treatment with GOMEKLI and for 1 week after the last dose.

Referenced NDAs, BLAs, DMFs
The Applicant’s Position:
There are no referenced NDAs, BLAs, or DMFs related to nonclinical pharmacology or toxicology
for mirdametinib.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees.

Pharmacology
The Applicant’s Position:
Mirdametinib (PD-0325901) has been evaluated in numerous in vitro and in vivo nonclinical
studies that were designed to characterize the pharmacology, safety pharmacology,
pharmacokinetics (PK) and toxicology of the compound. The pharmacological properties of
mirdametinib have been studied using enzyme and cell-based assays and evaluated in tumor
xenograft models and in an NF1 mouse model. Biochemical assays have shown that
mirdametinib is a selective, noncompetitive, allosteric inhibitor of mitogen-activated protein
kinase kinase (MEK)-1 and MEK2 and thereby inhibits phosphorylation of the extracellular
signal-regulated kinase (ERK)1 and ERK2, which in turn impairs tumor cell growth through the
MAPK pathway.
Primary Pharmacology
As a potent MEK inhibitor, mirdametinib has an inhibition constant (Ki) for the activated forms
ŽĨ D<ϭ ĂŶĚ D<Ϯ ŽĨ ч ϭ͘ϭ nM. In a cell-based assay measuring inhibition of phosphorylated
ERK1 as a measure of MEK inhibition of the Ras/Raf/MEK/ERK pathway, the Ki for mirdametinib
was 0.90 nM.
The specificity for various species was determined by comparing the potency of mirdametinib
in cell-based assays using Colon-26 (mouse), MDCK (dog), and HCT-116 (human) cancer cell
lines. The pharmacodynamic properties of mirdametinib are summarized in Table 2.

The minimally effective dose (MED) of mirdametinib was evaluated in NF1 mice by treating
mice with established tumors at the relatively low doses 0.5 and 1.5 mg/kg/day (Jousma 2015).
In this study, 0.5 mg/kg dose group (human equivalent dose of approximately 2.5 mg twice
daily [BID]) led to statistically significant neurofibroma shrinkage as compared to vehicletreated mice. As shown in Figure 2, the extent of shrinkage seen in the 0.5 mg/kg mirdametinib
group was comparable to the shrinkage of tumors from mice treated with mirdametinib at
1.5 mg/kg (dose was noted to provide a concentration similar to 8 mg BID in humans)
(Jessen 2013). The low dose (0.5 mg/kg) of mirdametinib led to comparable reductions in tumor

Mirdametinib is a potent MEK inhibitor, as demonstrated in a variety of species and nonclinical
models. Evaluation in mouse models of NF1 that have similar histopathology and growth rates
as human NF1 PNs demonstrated antitumor activity of mirdametinib treatment in NF1. Doseranging studies in NF1 mouse models also support the use of low doses of mirdametinib in
achieving tumor reductions and demonstrate that NF1 neurofibromas have high sensitivity to
MEK inhibition.
The FDA’s Assessment:
In general, FDA agrees with the Applicant’s conclusions. However, the Applicant uses
terminology such as “selectively and efficiently inhibits,” “superior,” “clear,” and “potent” to
describe study results; such terms should be avoided as they are vague, subjective, and
promotional. In addition to these findings, the FDA notes the following results:
M22, a unique human metabolite of mirdametinib, demonstrated considerably lower ability to
reduce ERK phosphorylation in HCT-116 cells (IC50 values of 362 nM to 835 nM). A carboxylic
acid metabolite of mirdametinib, PD-0315209, also demonstrated activity as assessed by
inhibition of MEK1 phosphorylation in a cell-based assay (IC50=1.3 μM); however, activity was
lower compared to mirdametinib (IC50=0.59 nM).

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the results presented by the Applicant. The increased respiratory rate and
decreased tidal volume noted in rats (Study# 745-ϬϯϴϬϵͿ ǁĞƌĞ ƵƉ ƚŽ ϯϵй ĂŶĚ ϮϬй͕
respectively. Of note, the Applicant did not include females in the stand-alone safety
pharmacology studies; however, effects on CNS, cardiovascular, and respiratory parameters
were evaluated in the repeat-dose toxicology studies in rats and dogs that included both males
and females. There were no effects on CNS parameters in rats at doses up to 1 mg/kg or dogs at
doses up to 0.4 mg/kg after repeated dosing for up to 3 months. Mineralization of the arteries
was noted in rats at 1 mg/kg in the 1-month and 3-month studies, but no cardiovascular effects
were noted in dogs at doses up to 0.4 mg/kg. Though minimal to mild foamy macrophage
infiltrates were noted in the lungs of rats at 1 mg/kg in the 3-month study, clinical signs of
changes in respiratory parameters were not noted in rats or dogs.

In dose range finding studies in rats (745-03478) and dogs (745-03537), orally
administered mirdametinib was rapidly absorbed and increased in a dose proportional
manner. Time of the maximum concentration (Tmax) was reached by 1 hour in rats and
ranged from 1 to 4 hours in dogs, with most animals reaching Tmax by 2 hours on Day 14.
Plasma levels were similar between males and females at all doses. PK of mirdametinib
was evaluated following oral and intravenous (IV) administration to male Sprague-Dawley
rats (764-04243), male and female beagle dogs (764-04241), and male cynomolgus
monkeys (764-04242). dŚĞ ĂďƐŽůƵƚĞ ďŝŽĂǀĂŝůĂďŝůŝƚǇ ǁĂƐ ŵŽĚĞƌĂƚĞ ƚŽ ŚŝŐŚ ;Ϯϵ͘ϱй͕ ϭϬϯй͕

plasma ratios were generally similar across species and did not show meaningful variability
with increasing drug concentration (764-04271).
Mirdametinib is preferentially distributed to plasma. The distribution of mirdametinib and
PD-0315209 into brain and CSF was evaluated in male rats following IV infusion of 1 mg/kg
over 30 minutes (19SPRWP11R1). The half-life of mirdametinib in the rat brain was longer
than in plasma and the brain to plasma ratio increased over time. PD-0315209 did not
appear to enter the rat brain.
A whole-body autoradiography study (764-04029) showed that radioactivity derived from
[14C] mirdametinib is widely distributed throughout the body with [14C] mirdametinib
equivalents in most tissues lower than in blood. For all tissues, the highest measured
concentrations were observed within 4 hours and 0.5 hours postdose for the oral and IV
routes, respectively. Drug equivalents were not retained in pigmented tissues. Distribution
of [14C] mirdametinib equivalents in brain demonstrated distinct regional localization with
highest levels in striatum, hippocampus, and olfactory bulb. [14C] mirdametinib equivalents
in thoracic duct was approximately equal to blood through 24 hours after dosing,
suggesting that drug equivalents may be recycled via intestinal lymphatics. Following IV
administration of [14C] mirdametinib to male Long Evans rats, the distribution of
[14C] mirdametinib equivalents was similar to that seen after oral dose administration.
Evidence of recirculation was not as distinct. In both oral- and IV-dosed animals, high
[14C] mirdametinib equivalents were present in bile ducts through the 24-hour collection

In vitro metabolism of mirdametinib is consistent across nonclinical species and humans.
All circulating metabolites observed in human liver microsomes were also present in rat,
dog, and monkey liver microsomes and hepatocytes (764-04904). [14C] mirdametinib
undergoes both glucuronidation and oxidation of the parent compound. A carboxylic acid
metabolite of mirdametinib (PD-0315209) was detected in microsomal incubations
without the addition of NADPH as well as following preincubation with
aminobenzotriazole, suggesting that this metabolite can be formed by non-CYP enzyme(s)
with very little contribution by a single CYP isoform being identified in an in vitro study
with recombinant enzymes. In vitro studies assessing the contribution of UGT to the
metabolism of mirdametinib (showed that metabolism of mirdametinib was significant for

;ϱϳ͘ϵйͿ͘  ƌĞaction phenotyping assay explored the contribution of CES to the metabolism

by CES1c, indicating that these enzymes may contribute to the conversion of mirdametinib
to the primary metabolite, PD-0315209.
Mass balance and metabolite identification studies were conducted in both rat (76405091) and dog (764-05213). Following oral administration of 3 mg/kg to rats,
[14C] mirdametinib underwent extensive metabolism via glucuronidation and oxidation.

and their safety margins relative to human exposure are provided in Table 4.

Abbreviations: AUC0-24: area under the plasma concentration-time curve from 0 to 24 hours; BID: twice
daily; EFD: embryo-fetal development; LOAEL: lowest-observed-adverse-effect level; MTD: maximum
tolerated dose, NOAEL: no -observed-adverse-effect level; popPK: population pharmacokinetic; TK:

a
The AUC values are an average of the reported male and female values at the end of the dosing period.
b
The PopPK report presents a mean steady-state AUC0-24 of 1862 ng·h/mL at 2 mg/m2 bid with a maximum
of 4 mg BID for mirdametinib.
c
The PopPK report presents a mean steady-state AUC0-24 of 1362 ng·h/mL for metabolite PD-0315209
when administered mirdametinib at 2 mg/m2 BD with maximum of 4 mg BID.
d
TKs were not assessed in this study; therefore, the exposure was derived from the 1-month rat study.

Mirdametinib is rapidly absorbed, with Tmax typically being observed within 1 to 2 hours of oral
administration. Exposure increases with dose in a generally dose proportional manner and
minimal accumulation is observed following repeat dosing for either mirdametinib or its
primary metabolite, PD-0315209. Absolute bioavailability of mirdametinib ranged from
moderate (rat and monkey) to high (dog). Mirdametinib showed high protein binding in all
evaluated species and was also found to be widely distributed into tissues, including exposure
in the brain. Mirdametinib is extensively metabolized and primarily undergoes glucuronidation
and oxidation of the parent compound. UGT1A6 and UGT2B7 are heavily involved in the
metabolism of mirdametinib and its most abundant metabolite, PD-0315209, is produced by
carboxylesterase variants CES1b and CES1c. The majority of the drug was excreted in the feces

Mirdametinib (GOMEKLI)
*Day 90 data not available for 0.4 mg/kg animals due to early deaths and stopping of dosing; H = hours

Toxicology
General Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
Mirdametinib was assessed in a series of nonclinical toxicology studies in alignment with
applicable International Conference on Harmonization (ICH) guidance documents. The oral
route of exposure was selected for these studies as the oral route is the intended clinical route
of administration. The rat and dog were selected as the general toxicology species based on
mirdametinib PK and metabolism properties compared across species. The definitive studies
were performed in the US in accordance with the US Department of Health and Human
Services, FDA, US Code of Federal Regulations, Title 21, Part 58: GLP for Nonclinical Laboratory
Studies.
Exploratory studies were conducted in mice, rats, dogs, and monkeys, while definitive toxicity
studies were conducted in rats and dogs. Studies were based on daily oral (gavage) dosing with
a limited number of studies involving IV administration. Mirdametinib was administered to rats
and dogs in repeat-dose general toxicology studies up to 3 months. The 1-month studies in rats
and dogs were followed by a 1-month recovery period. A 1-month mouse study was conducted
to support a 6-month carcinogenicity study in transgenic mice. A 2-year rat carcinogenicity
study will be conducted as a postmarking commitment. Preliminary embryo-fetal development
(EFD) toxicity studies were conducted in rats and rabbits, and a definitive fertility study was
conducted in male and female rats. Juvenile animal studies were not conducted.

PD-0325901 – Day 28

PD-0315209- Day 28

Findings
Died or Sacrificed
Moribund

Mortality was observed at 100 mg/kg/day beginning on Day 5, with all main phase and TK
animals found dead, euthanized moribund, or subjected to unscheduled euthanasia by the
end of Day 7.

Body Weight and
Food Consumption

Body weight gain was increased in male rats at 10 and 30 mg/kg/day and increased in
female rats at 10 mg/kg/day compared to controls. Mean body weight gain and Day 28 body
weights were similar in females at the 30 mg/kg/day dose. Differences in food consumption
were inconsistent. Due to early death or euthanasia, the 100 mg/kg/day mice were only
weighed on Day 4.

No Noteworthy
Findings

Gross findings

Clinical Observations

Dehydration, abnormal respiratory rate, hunched posture, closed eyes, cold to the touch,
and decreased activity in the 100 mg/kg/day dose group. There were no mirdametinib
related clinical observations in the 10 and 30 mg/kg/day groups.

Hematology

Increases in neutrophils and large unstained cells in males and females and increases in
monocytes in males at 10 and 30 mg/kg/day. Minimal to mild decreases in red cell mass,
hemoglobin, and hematocrit, coupled with increases in mean corpuscular volume, red cell
distribution width, platelets and reticulocytes at 30 mg/kg/day in males and females.

Clinical Chemistry

Non-adverse increases in ASP, ALT, ALP, globulin at 10 and 30 mg/kg/day males and
females. Decreases in albumin in females at 30/mg/kg/day and non-adverse decreases in
the albumin/globulin ratio in males and females at 10 and 30 mg/kg/day

Organ Weight

Increased thyroid gland weights in males and females at 30 mg/kg/day, increased spleen
weights in males and females at 30 mg/kg/day, decreased ovary weights at 10 and 30
mg/kg/day, and increased uterus weights at 10 and 30 mg/kg/day.

at 0.3 mg/kg. Hypoactivity was seen periodically during the dosing period in 3 males and 2
females at 0.3 mg/kg.

Abbreviations: AUC: area under the curve; Cmax: maximum plasma concentration; eCTD: electronic Common
Technical Document; F: female; GLP: Good Laboratory Practice; M: male; NA: not applicable; NOAEL: no
observed adverse effect level.; RBC: red blood cells
a Two males and 2 females at the 0.4 mg/kg/day dose were euthanized between Days 33 and 50 due to moribund
health status that included adverse clinical signs (decreased activity, tremor, titubation/impaired balance, low
head resulting in prostration, decreased food consumption, diarrhea, emesis and soft stool). Treatment was
suspended for the remaining high dose male on Day 51 and the high dose female on Day 57 until the end of the
study.
Microscopic
Observations

The primary mirdametinib toxicities in exploratory and definitive nonclinical studies were to the
gastrointestinal tract, skin, cornea, liver, bone, and systemic mineralization. Many of these
target organ effects can be attributed to the pharmacological mechanism of mirdametinib.
Intestinal toxicity was present in rats, dogs, monkeys, and rabbits. In dogs and monkeys,
erosions or ulcerations of the intestinal mucosa, or both, was dose limiting. These effects are
related to the requirement of the MEK pathway on normal intestinal function; MEK inhibition
manifests in ulcerations in intestinal mucosa in dogs and monkeys and translates to intestinal
toxicities in humans treated with MEK inhibitors (Mourad 2019). Skin lesions, characterized
microscopically as epidermal ulceration, were observed in rats in the 1- and 3-month studies.
Similar skin lesions were observed in the 1-month dog study, and in an exploratory 14-day
monkey study. These skin effects are expected based on the role of MEK on skin keratinocyte
survival (Jost 2001) and is a class effect with skin lesions noted in all approved MEK inhibitors.
Mirdametinib caused several toxicities that were specific to the rat. For example, in the
3-month rat study, corneal opacities were detected mainly in males with the incidence being
dose dependent. The fact that corneal opacities were detected almost exclusively in male rats
in the 3-month study suggests that tissue mineral deposition was the likely cause. Despite
inhibition of pERK in the repeat dose dog studies, corneal opacities were not observed.
Mineralization of vasculature and various soft tissues (systemic mineralization) was observed in
toxicology studies in rats in a time- and dose-dependent manner. However, these effects were
not seen in the dog or monkey. The stomach appears to be the most sensitive tissue since
mineralization of gastric fundic mucosa occurs prior to the onset of mineralization in other
tissues. Male rats were consistently more sensitive to this toxicity compared to female rats and
the effect was reversible. Administration of mirdametinib to rats resulted in bone lesions that
70

Mirdametinib (GOMEKLI)
included necrosis of the metaphysis and the ossifying zone of the physis and thickening of the
zone of hypertrophying cartilage of the physis. These changes were characterized by localized
injury to bone that appear to be due to local ischemia and/or necrosis. In the 1- and 3-month
rat studies, hypocellularity in the metaphyseal region of the distal portion of the femur and/or
proximal portion of the tibia occurred in males and was reversible following cessation of dosing
in the 1-month study. In addition, thickening of the bony trabeculae in the femoral/tibial
metaphyseal region was evident in males in the 3-month study. Bone lesions, like those
observed in rats, have not been seen in mice, dogs, or monkeys administered mirdametinib.
Safety margins are presented in Table 4.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA’s assessment is limited to repeat-dose general toxicology studies, and other studies, such
as carcinogenicity and embryo-fetal development, are discussed in the appropriate sections.
FDA did not verify the monkey findings discussed by the Applicant because this study had
limited number of animals and was non-GLP.
Studies # 20196649, 745-03826, 05067, 745-03833, and 05039 were previously reviewed by
FDA under IND 139883. In general, FDA agrees with most of the Applicant’s assessment of the
results of the GLP-compliant toxicity studies in rats and dogs. FDA notes that Study# 05039 in
dogs was 13-weeks in duration. The following summarizes FDA’s perspective of repeat-dose
studies results and indicates additional findings or differences of interpretation of results from
the Applicant, where appropriate.
Common clinical signs in early death dogs at 0.4 mg/kg in the 3-month study included diarrhea,
emesis, and soft stools that correlated with decreased food consumption and body weight loss;
clinical signs of emesis, abnormal stools were also noted at doses шϬ͘ϭ ŵŐͬŬŐ ŝŶ ƚŚĞ ϯ-month
dog study. Additionally, neutrophilic infiltrates/inflammation in the tongue, stomach, and/or
esophagus, as well as lymphoid depletion in multiple lymphoid organs and myeloid cell
hyperplasia in the bone marrow were noted in early death dogs, suggesting a systemic
inflammatory response.

Genetic Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
Data:
The genotoxicity of mirdametinib was assessed in a battery of genotoxicity studies as
summarized in Table 10. Mirdametinib was not mutagenic in bacteria (745-03535) and was not
clastogenic in human lymphocytes in vitro (745-03511). In an in vivo rat micronucleus study
mirdametinib was not clastogenic (745-03804). Additionally, in a rat bone marrow
chromosomal aberration assay, mirdametinib was negative for induction of structural or
numerical chromosome breaks in the treatment groups (745-04155).

Mirdametinib (GOMEKLI)

6 Clinical Pharmacology

Executive Summary
The FDA’s Assessment:
The clinical pharmacology key review questions focused on the evaluations of the proposed
dosage (including the two dosage forms and the unified dosing nomogram), food effect, and
drug-drug interactions and organ impairment.
The primary evidence for efficacy and safety was obtained from Study MEK-NF-201 which
ĞǀĂůƵĂƚĞĚ ŵŝƌĚĂŵĞƚŝŶŝď ŝŶ ƉĂƚŝĞŶƚƐ ш Ϯ ǇĞĂƌƐ ŽĨ ĂŐĞ ǁŝƚŚ ĂŶ ŝŶŽƉĞƌĂďůĞ E&ϭ-associated PN
causing significant morbidity. The proposed recommended dosage is 2 mg/m2 BID (with or
without food) for the first 21 days of each 28-day cycle (maximum dose is 4 mg BID) until
disease progression or unacceptable toxicity. The proposed dosage forms are capsules (1, 2 mg)
and tablets for oral suspension (1 mg). The tablets may be swallowed whole or dispersed in
water to be administered as an oral suspension to facilitate administration in patients who
cannot swallow pills.
The proposed recommended dosage is acceptable, based on the observed benefit-to-risk
profiles of mirdametinib from pivotal Study MEK-NF-201 and supportive Study NF-106. Based
on the clinical pharmacology review, the proposed dosage forms of capsules (1, 2 mg) and
tablets (1 mg) are acceptable given the overlapping PK observed between adult and pediatric
patients following administration of 2 mg/m2 BID via tablets or capsules, including comparable
PK in the overlapping ages of pediatric patients who received tablets vs. capsules (age 5 to 14
years). Efficacy was observed across pediatric age groups (2 to < 12, 12 to < 18 years), while
differences in TEAEs by dosage form appear attributable to age-related development
differences. The proposed dosing nomogram is acceptable, based on the PPK simulation which
showed no expected clinically meaningful differences in systemic exposures.
Based on the clinical pharmacology review of the in vitro and clinical studies, administration
with regard to food, DDIs and organ impairment were adequately addressed, except for the
following:
x The effects of moderate and severe hepatic impairment on the PK of mirdametinib is
not known, and a post-marketing requirement (PMR) will be issued to provide dosage
recommendations for mirdametinib in patients with moderate and severe hepatic
impairment.
x The effect of concomitant strong CYP3A4 inducers on the PK of mirdametinib is not
known. A post-marketing commitment (PMC) will be issued to provide dosage
recommendations for mirdametinib when used concomitantly with strong CYP3A4

Mirdametinib (GOMEKLI)

Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
Data:
Mirdametinib PK has been evaluated in 7 clinical studies as a single agent either as a single dose
(4 to 20 mg) or multiple doses (1 mg QD or BID up to 30 mg BID). Single dose PK studies were
conducted in healthy adult participants to evaluate the effects of food on mirdametinib PK
(Study A4581004), metabolism and elimination via radiolabeled mass balance (Study MEK-NF101), and effects of mirdametinib and PD-0315209 on QT interval (Study MEK-NF-102). Doseranging studies and pharmacodynamic (PDx) data were collected in adult participants with
advanced cancers (Study A4581001) and with non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC; Study
A4581002). PK and PDx data were collected from adult and pediatric participants with NF1 PN
at the target dose of 2 mg/m2 BID in Studies NF-106 and MEK-NF-201.
Collectively, these studies were used to characterize the PK of mirdametinib with population PK
(PopPK) and population pharmacokinetic pharmacodynamic (PopPKPDx) models to evaluate
exposure-response relationships with safety and efficacy endpoints. The exposure-efficacy and
exposure-safety analyses were used to support the recommendation of 2 mg/m2 BID, with a
maximum of 4 mg BID, administered orally for 3 weeks on and 1 week off (treatment for the
first 21 days of a 28- day cycle) as an optimal regimen of mirdametinib for the treatment of NF1
PN.
No clinical drug-drug interaction studies were conducted due to the absence of expected risk
based on nonclinical evaluations. Studies of the effects of severe renal impairment and
moderate or severe hepatic impairment have also not been conducted.
Mirdametinib has two major metabolites and both show less pharmacological activity than
mirdametinib. PD-0315209, Ă ĐĂƌďŽǆǇůŝĐ ĂĐŝĚ ŵĞƚĂďŽůŝƚĞ ƌĞƉƌĞƐĞŶƚŝŶŐ Ϯϰй ŽĨ ĐŝƌĐƵůĂƚŝŶŐ ĚƌƵŐrelated material, was significantly less effective than mirdametinib (approximately 2200-fold
difference) at reducing ERK phosphorylation in tumor cells grown in culture. PD-0315209
transiently suppressed pERK in tumor bearing mice; however, the PopPK predicted steady-state
maximum concentration (Cmax,ss) for PD-0315209 at the proposed target dose of 2 mg/m2 BID
is approximately 8-fold lower than the half-maximal inhibitory concentration (IC50) for pERK
suppression. M22, a secondary O-glucuronide representing ϭϰ͘ϳй ŽĨ ĐŝƌĐƵůĂƚŝŶŐ ĚƌƵŐ-related
material, was significantly less effective than mirdametinib (greater than 75-fold difference) at
reducing ERK phosphorylation in tumor cells grown in culture. Neither metabolite is expected to
have contributions to clinical efficacy.
The Applicant’s Position:
The clinical pharmacology studies support the recommended dose for mirdametinib of 2 mg/m2
BID with cycles of 3 weeks on followed by 1 week off treatment (treatment for the first 21 days

of a 28-day cycle). The BSA-driven dosing guidance ensures that PK between adult and pediatric
populations are similar. The metabolites are not expected to have clinically relevant
pharmacological activity.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position.
General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
6.2.2.1.

General Dosing

Data:
The selection of mirdametinib at 2 mg/m2 BID, with a maximum dose of 4 mg BID, on a 3 weeks
on/1 week off schedule as the Recommended Phase 2 Dose (RP2D) was made using the totality
of evidence from nonclinical data in the human tumor xenograft and mouse NF1 neurofibroma
models, tolerability assessment, PK and PDx measurements from clinical studies A4581001 and
A4581002 in advanced cancer patients, and findings from a Phase 2 investigator-initiated study
(NF-106) in NF1 PN.
The nonclinical PKPDx relationship was evaluated using mouse and human tumor xenograft
models, including an NF1 neurofibroma model. These models demonstrated human exposures
at the proposed dose of 2 mg/m2 BID were consistent with doses associated with pERK
ŝŶŚŝďŝƚŝŽŶ ŝŶ ŶŽŶĐůŝŶŝĐĂů ŵŽĚĞůƐ͕ ǁŚŝĐŚ ĚĞŵŽŶƐƚƌĂƚĞĚ ƚƵŵŽƌ ǀŽůƵŵĞ ƌĞĚƵĐƚŝŽŶƐ ŝŶ ϴϱй ŽĨ
treated mice in the NF1 mouse model.
In clinical studies of advanced cancer populations (Studies A4581001 and A4581002),
mirdametinib was assessed at various dose levels (1 mg QD to 30 mg BID) and schedules
(continuous, intermittent). Based on the occurrence of late-onset adverse events (AEs), dose
de-escalation, inclusive of both reduction of the total daily dose as well as the utilization of
different administration schedules, was explored. Due to challenges with tolerability at the
initial RP2D (15 mg BID) preventing long-term administration, full evaluation of mirdametinib
efficacy in participants with advanced cancers could not be performed. It was concluded that an
intermittent administration schedule using doses between 2 and 10 mg BID should be explored
to identify a recommended dose for long-term treatment with mirdametinib (LoRusso 2010). In
Study NF-ϭϬϲ͕ ƉĂƌƚŝĐŝƉĂŶƚƐ шϭϲ ǇĞĂƌƐ ŽĨ ĂŐĞ ǁŝƚŚ E&ϭ ĂŶĚ ƉƌŽŐƌĞƐƐŝŶŐ ĂŶĚͬŽƌ ŵŽƌďŝĚ WE
received mirdametinib at a 2 mg/m2 dose (maximum 4 mg BID) on a 3 weeks on/1 week off
schedule. In this study, mirdametiŶŝď ǁĂƐ ǁĞůů ƚŽůĞƌĂƚĞĚ ĂŶĚ ƌĞƐƵůƚĞĚ ŝŶ Ă ĐŽŶĨŝƌŵĞĚ KZZ хϯϬй͘
Based on these supportive data, a mirdametinib dose of 2 mg/m2 (maximum 4 mg BID) on a
3 weeks on/1 week off schedule was selected for the pivotal Phase 2b Study MEK-NF-201.
The results of exposure-response analyses identified a clear relationship between exposure and
tumor growth, with increasing average concentration at steady-state being predictive of
95
Version date: June 2022 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5528369

NDA/IND Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 219379/219389
Mirdametinib (GOMEKLI)
increasing reduction in tumor volume, increasing probability of response, and reduced time to
response. Exposure-safety analyses identified relationships between mirdametinib and
PD-0315209 exposure and incidence of several AEs. No relationships with AEs of Grade ш2
severity were identified.
The actual body surface area (BSAs) and doses used in the PopPK and PKPDx modeling datasets
were of a sufficiently wide range to support simulation of PK and tumor response across a
range of doses. The median (minimum, maximum) of actual doses was 2.52 mg/m2 (0.424, 18.6)
in the PopPK model development and 2.21 mg/m2 (0.806, 2.86) in participants with NF1 PN in
Studies MEK-NF-201 and NF-106, which provided tumor volume measurements (Figure 4).
Therefore, the range of actual doses administered in the studies used to develop these models
supports their predictive ability to estimate safety and efficacy responses at target
mirdametinib doses ranging from approximately 1.0 to 3.0 mg/m2.

Histogram includes all non-zero actual doses corresponding to a tumor volume scan in Study MEK-NF-201 and NF106 participants. The dashed vertical green lines indicate the interquartile range of actual doses.
Source: Derived from ADEX

Population and trial simulations were conducted using exposure-efficacy and exposure-safety
models to predict the impact of various target dose levels from 1.0 to 3.0 mg/m2 BID (1.0, 1.5,
2.0, 2.2, and 3.0 mg/m2). The simulations support that exposures associated with the 2 mg/m2
dose of mirdametinib provide an optimal balance of robust efficacy for patients with NF1 PN
with a tolerable incidence of low severity exposure-related AEs.
Population simulations were also conducted to evaluate the impact of a simplified nomogram
for use with both tablet and capsule formulations. Study MEK-NF-201 used separate

Mirdametinib (GOMEKLI)
6.2.2.2.

Therapeutic Individualization

Data:
Body weight scaled allometrically with clearance and volume of distribution in a PopPK analysis.
BSA, however, was not identified as a covariate that affects mirdametinib PK. The lack of
association with BSA is expected based on the use of BSA-based guidance for selecting the
administered dose. Simulations of PK by administered dose identified that PK is largely
overlapping regardless of administered dose.
The age range in the PopPK analysis was from 2 through 86 years of age, however few
participants over the age of 65 were enrolled in the studies. No clinically meaningful effects on
mirdametinib PK were identified for age, sex, race, or baseline liver function tests. No
meaningful relationships were identified for participants with creatinine clearance values
consistent with mild or moderate renal impairment or for participants with bilirubin values
consistent with mild hepatic impairment based on the NCI-organ dysfunction working group
(ODWG) criteria.
The Applicant’s Position:
Therapeutic individualization based on BSA is proposed using the simplified nomogram
supported by the PopPK and PKPDx simulations (Table 14). Individualization of dosing is not
required for other demographic factors (age, sex, race), for patients with mild or moderate
renal impairment, or for patients with mild hepatic impairment (based on NCI-ODWG). Effects
of severe renal impairment or end stage renal disease (ERSD) cannot be estimated from the
PopPK analysis. Potential effects of moderate or severe hepatic impairment will be studied in a
planned hepatic impairment study.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. A PMR for renal impairment is not needed, given that
mirdametinib is minimally renally eliminated ;фϭ͘ϱй ŵŝƌĚĂŵĞƚŝŶŝď ĂŶĚ W-0315209 recovered
in the urine).
Outstanding Issues
The presented clinical pharmacology program adequately characterizes the PK of mirdametinib
and evaluates the need for dosing guidance based on intrinsic/extrinsic factors. Nonclinical
evaluations of drug-drug interaction potential suggest low clinical risk for major CYP-mediated
interactions or transporters of clinical interest.
An additional clinical study is planned to evaluate the impacts of moderate or severe hepatic
impairment on mirdametinib PK.

difference) at reducing ERK phosphorylation in tumor cells grown in culture and, as a Phase 2
conjugation reaction generally renders a compound more water soluble and pharmacologically
inactive, no further evaluation was warranted. Neither metabolite is expected to have a
substantial contribution to pharmacological activity of mirdametinib.

Renal Impairment: Total urinary elimination of mirdametinib and PD-ϬϯϭϱϮϬϵ ǁĂƐ ϭ͘ϯй ŽĨ ƚŚĞ
administered drug (Study MEK-NF-101). In the regulatory guidance on PK in renal impairment
(FDA 2020), a drug is considered to be substantially eliminated by the renal route when the
fraction of systemically available drug or active metabolite eliminated iŶ ƚŚĞ ƵƌŝŶĞ ŝƐ хϯϬй͘
Therefore, effects of renal impairment on the PK of mirdametinib have not been evaluated in a
dedicated clinical study. Of note, the enrollment of participants with CLCR indicative of mild
renal impairment was allowed in Study MEK NF-201. Additionally, participants with moderate
renal impairment were included in Studies A4581001 and A4581002. Covariate analysis for
baseline creatinine clearance (CLCR) was conducted in the PopPK analysis. The PopPK analysis
population contained 60 participants with CLCR consistent with mild impairment and 10
participants with moderate impairment. No relationship between mirdametinib PK and CLCR
was observed. A statistically significant effect was found for CLCR on the clearance of PD0315209 (CLm/F), suggesting a decrease in CLm/F with increasing severity of renal impairment.
PopPK model predicted exposures of PD-0315209 for participants with normal function, mild
renal impairment, or moderate renal impairment suggest an increase in exposure to PD0315209 in participants with moderate renal impairment; however, the exposures remain far
below concentrations associated with pERK inhibition. Participants with severe renal
impairment and ESRD were not represented in the PopPK analysis and expected effects to
mirdametinib and PD-0315209 exposure cannot be extrapolated.
Hepatic Impairment: The effects of hepatic impairment on the PK of mirdametinib have not
been evaluated in a dedicated clinical study. Covariate analysis for baseline liver function tests
was conducted in the PopPK analysis. Based on baseline bilirubin values and the NCI-ODWG
classification system, 19 participants in the PopPK analysis population were consistent with
mild impairment (Group B2) and 6 were consistent with moderate impairment (Group C). No
clinically meaningful relationships were observed between mirdametinib or PD-0315209 PK and
baseline liver enzymes levels (aspartate aminotransferase [AST], alanine aminotransferase
[ALT], and Bilirubin). The effects of moderate and severe hepatic impairment cannot be inferred
from the PopPK analysis. A dedicated clinical study in these populations is planned as a PMC.
Victim DDI: Mirdametinib is a substrate of P-glycoprotein (P-gp) and breast cancer resistance
protein (BCRP). PD-0315209 is a substrate of BCRP. Concomitant use of P-gp inhibitors showed
no impact on PopPK model predicted exposures of mirdametinib and PD-0315209. No
dedicated clinical studies have been conducted to assess the risk of victim DDIs from P-gp or
BCRP inhibitors.
Nonclinical studies suggest minimal risk for DDIs from inhibitors or inducers of CYP-mediated
metabolism. Mirdametinib is a substrate of UGT and CES enzymes. PD-0315209 is a substrate of
UGT enzymes. Concomitant use of UGT1A6 inhibitors and inducers, UGT2B7 inhibitors and
inducers, and CES inhibitors showed no impact on PopPK model predicted exposures of

6.3.2.2 Is the proposed dosing regimen appropriate for the general patient
population for which the indication is being sought?
Data:
Yes. In the PopPK analysis, no clinically meaningful covariate effects for sex, race, or disease
status were found. An allometric effect for weight on the clearance and volumes of distribution
for mirdametinib and PD-0315209 was found; the BSA-driven dosing adequately controls for
substantial variance across the range of actual doses administered. Most AEs were managed by
standard supportive care measures and/or dose modifications based on BSA.
The Applicant’s Position:
The proposed BSA-based dosing regimen of 2 mg/m2 BID, with a maximum dose of 4 mg BID, on
a 3 weeks on/1 week off schedule is suitable for both adult and pediatric patients with NF1 PN.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. The proposed recommended dosage is acceptable,
based on the observed benefit-to-risk profiles of mirdametinib from pivotal Study MEK-NF-201
and supportive Study NF-106.
6.3.2.3 Is an alternative dosing regimen or management strategy required for
subpopulations based on intrinsic patient factors (e.g. race, ethnicity, age,
performance status, genetic subpopulations, etc.)?
Data:
The effects of hepatic impairment on the PK of mirdametinib were not evaluated in a dedicated
clinical study. Covariate analysis for baseline liver function tests was conducted in the PopPK
analysis. Based on baseline bilirubin values and the NCI-ODWG classification system,
19 participants in the PopPK analysis population were consistent with mild impairment
(Group B2) and 6 were consistent with moderate impairment (Group C). No clinically
meaningful relationships were found between mirdametinib PK and baseline liver enzymes
levels (AST, ALT, and bilirubin). The effects of moderate and severe hepatic impairment cannot
be inferred from the PopPK analysis. A dedicated clinical study in these populations is planned.
The effects of renal impairment on the PK of mirdametinib have not been evaluated in a
dedicated clinical study. Covariate analysis for baseline CLCR was conducted in the PopPK
analysis. The PopPK analysis population contained 60 participants with CLCR consistent with
mild impairment and 10 participants with moderate impairment. No relationship between
mirdametinib PK and CLCR was observed. PopPK-predicted Cmax, Ctrough, and AUC for
mirdametinib were not meaningfully different between participants with normal function, mild
renal impairment, or moderate renal impairment. No evaluations have been conducted with

6.3.2.4 Are there clinically relevant food-drug or drug-drug interactions, and what is
the appropriate management strategy?
Data:
Food Effect: Study A4581004 evaluated the effect of a high-fat, high-calorie meal on
mirdametinib PK in healthy adult participants receiving a single oral 20 mg dose of
mirdametinib. The dose evaluated in this study is higher than the proposed dose of 2 mg/m2
BID for NF1 PN because it was conducted at a dose hypothesized to be relevant to the
advanced cancer indication under development at the time. PopPK analysis supports dose
proportionality up to at least 20 mg, indicating that results from this study are applicable to the
2 mg/m2 dose level. Consumption of a high-fat, high-calorie meal reduced and delayed the rate
of absorption (ϰϯй ƌĞĚƵĐƚŝŽŶ ŝŶ ŵĂǆ; Tmax delayed by 2 hours). The extent of absorption was
slightly reduced (AUCŝŶĨ ƌĞĚƵĐĞĚ ďǇ ϳйͿ, but the confidence intervals of the geometric leastsquares mean ratio (GLMR) remained within standard bioequivalence limits. Mirdametinib was
dosed without regard to food in Study MEK-NF-201 and NF-106.
Exposure-response analyses concluded that average concentration, or AUC, is the primary
driver of efficacy for mirdametinib. Therefore, a reduction in Cmax with maintenance of the
overall extent of exposure is not considered clinically meaningful for mirdametinib.
Mirdametinib may be dosed without regard to food.
Gastric acid modifying agents: Mirdametinib is highly soluble across the physiological pH range.
Dissolution data also support rapid dissolution of all strengths of both tablet and capsule dose
forms across the physiological pH range. Therefore, drugs that modify gastric pH are not
expected to impact the dissolution or solubility of mirdametinib and represent a low risk of
altering its clinical exposure. Mirdametinib may be taken concomitantly with gastric acid
reducing agents.
Victim DDI: Mirdametinib is a substrate of P-gp and BCRP. PD-0315209 is a substrate of BCRP.
Concomitant use of P-gp inhibitors showed no impact on PopPK model predicted exposures of
mirdametinib and PD-0315209. No dedicated clinical studies have been conducted to assess the
risk of victim DDIs from P-gp or BCRP inhibitors.
Nonclinical studies suggest minimal risk for DDIs from inhibitors or inducers of CYP-mediated
metabolism. Mirdametinib is a substrate of UGT and CES enzymes. PD-0315209 is a substrate of
UGT enzymes. Concomitant use of UGT1A6 inhibitors and inducers, UGT2B7 inhibitors and
inducers, and CES inhibitors showed no impact on PopPK model predicted exposures of
mirdametinib. An effect of UGT2B7 inducers was observed on the Fm to PD-0315209, leading to
Ă ϭϵй ŝŶĐƌĞĂƐĞ ŝŶ &ŵ͘ DŽĚĞů ƉƌĞĚŝĐƚĞĚ ĞǆƉŽƐƵƌĞƐ ĨŽƌ W-0315209 were still comparable in
participants with and without concomitant use of UGT2B7 inducers.
Taken together, mirdametinib is unlikely to be susceptible to CYP- or transporter-mediated DDIs

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position and notes that the primary analysis of efficacy and
safety was based on results from Study MEK-NF-201. The Applicant submitted a complete data
set for this study, using a data cut date of September 20, 2023, at the time of the initial NDA
submission. The Applicant submitted an updated data set, with DCO of June 12, 2024, at the
time of the 90-day safety update (submitted September 26, 2024, to SDN 19). The updated data
from Study MEK-NF-201 will form the basis for this review.
Efficacy from Study NF-106 was evaluated as supportive evidence given differences in the study
design that did not allow for full evaluation of ORR and DOR, with patients being required to
discontinue treatment if they did not have a response by a certain timepoint. Results from this
trial were not independently verified during the review of this application.
Study MEK-NF-201 was open-label and did not include a comparator arm; therefore, the
analyses of efficacy and safety are descriptive only. Where possible, confidence intervals are
provided to assist in the interpretation of the efficacy data. For additional statistical
methodologies, see Section 8.1.1.

8 Statistical and Clinical Evaluation
Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
Studies MEK-NF-201 and NF-106 are the relevant studies used to support efficacy.
Study MEK-NF-201
Study Design
The Applicant’s Description:
Study MEK-NF-201 is an open-label, multi-center, Phase 2b study being conducted to determine
ƚŚĞ ĞĨĨŝĐĂĐǇ ĂŶĚ ƐĂĨĞƚǇ ŽĨ ŵŝƌĚĂŵĞƚŝŶŝď ŝŶ ƉĂƌƚŝĐŝƉĂŶƚƐ шϮ ǇĞĂƌƐ ŽůĚ ǁŝƚŚ ƐǇŵƉƚŽŵĂƚŝĐ
inoperable NF1-associated PNs. A total of 113 participants enrolled and stratified by age
(approximately 50 participants 2 to 17 years of age and 50 participants ш 18 years of age).
Mirdametinib was orally administered at a dose of 2 mg/m2 BID (maximum 4 mg BID) in 4-week
cycles on an intermittent (3 weeks on/1 week off) schedule. This study consists of two phases:
the Treatment Phase (24 cycles) and the optional Long Term Follow Up (LTFU) Phase.
Participants who completed Cycle 24 of the Treatment Phase and did not meet withdrawal
criteria were eligible to participate in the LTFU Phase. Withdrawal criteria included the
participant having met the definition of progressive disease, occurrence of a medical condition
or circumstance that exposed participant to substantial risk or prevented the participant from
adhering to protocol requirements, any SAE which the investigator considered was not in the
best interest of the participant to continue, pregnancy, prohibited concomitant medication,
failure to comply with protocol requirements, or termination of the study by the Sponsor, IRB
or regulatory authority. Eligible participants will continue taking the last dose assigned in the
Treatment Phase. Participants will remain in the LTFU Phase until a withdrawal criterion is met
or mirdametinib is commercially available. The treatment phase of the study is completed; the
LTFU phase of the study is currently ongoing.

The primary endpoint was confirmed ORR, defined as the proportion of participants who had a
ĐŽŶĨŝƌŵĞĚ шϮϬй ƌĞĚƵĐƚŝŽŶ ŝŶ ƚĂƌŐĞƚ ƚƵŵŽƌ ǀŽůƵŵĞ ĐŽŵƉĂƌĞĚ ƚŽ ďĂƐĞůŝŶĞ ĂƐ ĂƐƐĞƐƐĞĚ ďǇ Ă
blinded independent central reviewer (BICR). The response needed to be confirmed in a
consecutive tumor assessment within 2 to 6 months during the treatment phase. All tumor
volume measurements were independently determined by 2 reviewers. In the case that the
two measurements represented different tumor response statuses, as defined above, an
independent adjudicator made the final decision on which response category is recorded at
that visit. The primary endpoint analysis was performed using the adjudicator-selected reader
for those visits where the two BICR readers did not agree. Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI)
was performed approximately every 4 cycles throughout the Treatment Phase and the LTFU
Phase.
Key secondary endpoints included duration of response (DoR), quality of life (QoL) and pain
questionnaires, and safety and tolerability of mirdametinib.
Administrative Structure
This study utilized an independent data monitoring committee (DMC) and operated according
to an established charter. The committee was composed of 3 members including physicians
knowledgeable in the treatment of NF1 and an independent statistician. Sponsor employees
were not members of the DMC. The DMC was responsible for ongoing monitoring of the safety,
efficacy and benefit/risk profile of participants in the Treatment Phase of the study. Reviews
included aggregate safety, targeted medical events of special interest, serious AE data and
aggregate endpoint data.
Study Location
A total of 50 sites were opened across the US.
External Control Data
As the natural history of NF1 PN is incompletely described in the published literature, notably
for the adult population, NF1 natural history data were gathered and analyzed to serve as an
external historical control to support this application.

Participants must have a target PN that is causing significant morbidity, such as (but not
limited to) head and neck lesions that are compromising the airway or great vessels,
brachial or lumbar plexus lesions that are causing nerve compression and loss of
function, lesions causing major deformity or are significantly disfiguring, lesions of the
extremity that cause limb hypertrophy or loss of function, and painful lesions.
Participant has a target PN that is deemed inoperable, defined as a PN that cannot be
completely surgically removed without risk for substantial morbidity due to:
encasement of or close proximity to vital structures, invasiveness, or high vascularity of
the PN, or the participant refuses surgery.
Participants must have a target PN, defined as the clinically most relevant PN, amenable
to volumetric MRI analysis. For the purpose of this study, the target PN must be seen on
at least 3 consecutive MRI slices and the field of view must contain the entire tumor of
interest.

All tumor volume measurements were independently calculated by two BICR readers. In the

tumor response statuses, an independent adjudicator made the final decision on which
reader’s assessment was the best representation of tumor response and the selected
response category was recorded at that visit. The primary endpoint analyses were
performed using the adjudicator-selected reader for those visits where the two BICR
readers did not agree.

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the description of the trial design and endpoints. Notably, under
Response Evaluation in Neurofibromatosis and Schwannomatosis (REiNS) criteria, a partial
response (PR) is defined as a decrease in the ǀŽůƵŵĞ ŽĨ ƚŚĞ ƚĂƌŐĞƚ ůĞƐŝŽŶ ďǇ ϮϬй Žƌ ŵŽƌĞ

maintained for at least 6 months. In contrast, the AƉƉůŝĐĂŶƚ ĐŽŶƐŝĚĞƌĞĚ Ă шϮϬй ĚĞĐƌĞĂƐĞ ŝŶ WE
from baseline across consecutive scans within 2–6 months. A sensitivity analysis was
conducted, which showed minimal impact on the ORR outcome in pediatric cohort and slightly
larger impact in adult cohort. The details of the rationale for the modified criteria and the
sensitivity analysis are provided in section 8.1.1.1.
FDA considers the external control data to be supportive in a descriptive manner and as such
the data from the placebo arm of Study 01-C-0222 and the natural history Study 08-C-0079
were not independently confirmed and will not be included in FDA’s assessment of efficacy.

Primary Efficacy Endpoint
The primary efficacy endpoint was the confirmed ORR by the end of Treatment Phase (i.e.,
Cycle 24). The primary endpoint was based on the full analysis set (FAS), defined as the set of all
participants who received at least one dose of mirdametinib. The confirmed ORR was defined
ĂƐ ƚŚĞ ƉƌŽƉŽƌƚŝŽŶ ŽĨ ƉĂƌƚŝĐŝƉĂŶƚƐ ǁŚŽ ŚĂĚ Ă ĐŽŶĨŝƌŵĞĚ шϮϬй ƌĞĚƵĐƚŝŽŶ ŝŶ ƚĂƌŐĞƚ ƚƵŵŽƌ ǀŽůƵŵĞ
as compared to baseline as assessed by a blinded independent central review (BICR), and the
response needs to be confirmed by BICR in a consecutive tumor assessment within 2- 6 months.
All tumor volume measurements were independently calculated by two BICR readers. In the
case that the two readers generated measurements representing different categorical tumor
response statuses, an independent adjudicator made the final decision on which reader’s
assessment was the best representation of tumor response and the selected response category
was recorded at that visit. The primary endpoint analyses were performed using the
adjudicator-selected reader for those visits where the two BICR readers did not agree.
Only tumor assessments during the Treatment Phase were considered for the determination of
confirmed response. Participants who did not achieve a confirmed response were categorized
as non-responders. Participants who did not have a post-baseline MRI assessment were also
categorized as non-responders and their best overall response was deemed as ‘Missing’.
To assess the primary efficacy endpoint, confirmed ORRs by the end of the Treatment Phase as
assessed by BICR were presented as percentages alongside the corresponding 2-ƐŝĚĞĚ ϵϱй

Duration of Response
Duration of response was defined as the time in months between the first instance of response
that was subsequently confirmed, until the date of radiographic disease progression or death,

whichever occurred first. The DoR was summarized in months as follows: (date of event [i.e.,

12.
For participants who entered the LTFU Phase, all MRI assessments in both the Treatment Phase
and LTFU Phase were used to determine DoR. Those who achieved a DoR of at least 12 months
were considered to have experienced a durable response.

ƋƵĂƌƚŝůĞ ĞƐƚŝŵĂƚĞƐ ŽĨ ŽZ ĂŶĚ ƚŚĞŝƌ ĂƐƐŽĐŝĂƚĞĚ ϵϱй CIs using the Brookmeyer and Crowley
method.
Patient Reported Outcomes
All PROs analyzed in this study were assessed using the mean change from baseline as well as
by the proportion of participants who achieved a clinically meaningful change from baseline at
the Cycle 13 visit. Cycle 13 was selected as the timepoint that will best allow the treatment
effect to be captured in PRO scores. It was projected that a strong majority of participants who
present a response would do so at or before Cycle 13, with the median time of response
expected to be Cycle 5 to Cycle 9, so any associated pain or QoL improvements should be
captured at the Cycle 13 visit. Choosing a later cycle would have captured few additional
responses, but may have been susceptible to increased amounts of missing data.
For PedsQL, distribution-based thresholds were >0.5*StD, where StD was calculated from the
baseline scale data. MCTs were defined separately for adult, pediatric self-report, and pediatric
parent proxy data.
For NRS-11, distribution-ďĂƐĞĚ ƚŚƌĞƐŚŽůĚƐ ǁĞƌĞ ƉƌĞƐĞŶƚĞĚ ƵƐŝŶŐ ĐŚĂŶŐĞ ĨƌŽŵ ďĂƐĞůŝŶĞ ŽĨ ч -1
(this cut point has previously been researched and assessed as a meaningful change in pediatric
populations), representing a clinically meaningful improvement, as well as change from
baseline greater than the within-patient MCT.
Distribution-based MCTs were applied to interpret the change from baseline results as well as
the number and percentage of participants with a clinically meaningful change for PII.
Distribution-based thresholds were < -0.5*StD (using StD from the baseline scores).

response status for each PROMIS Scale, with self -report and parent proxy records summarized

separately.
Strength
The strength measured by the dynamometer by each area location was summarized and the
percent change from baseline using standard summary statistics was summarized by age cohort
and by visit.
Range of Motion
The range of motion for that location was summarized and the percent change using standard
summary statistics was summarized by age cohort and by visit.
6-Minute Walk Test
Standard summary statistics of length walked and the pre-/post vitals were summarized by
each visit.

Time to response was summarized for confirmed responders by age cohort and is defined as
the time in months from the start of treatment to the date of first response that was
subsequently confirmed. The time to response was summarized for the primary endpoint and
by each of the two readers separately for the FAS.

External Control
Yearly Percentage Change from Baseline in Target PN Volume
For each cohort, an analysis of covariance (ANCOVA) model was applied for treatment
comparison between participants in MEK-NF-201 and the historical control at each year.
Comparisons of this endpoint were made at each year up to 3 years. Since the 2 studies for
historical controls did not have the same assessment timepoints as in MEK-NF-201, a window of
±6 months was used for the comparison timepoints at months 12, 24, 36, etc. This rule created
the following windows in months for comparison:
x
x
x
x

Month 12: (6, 18]
Month 24: (18, 30]
Month 36: (30, 42]
Last assessment time point.

Within each window, the assessment timepoint closest to the yearly comparison timepoint was
selected to calculate the percentage change from baseline in target PN volume. Comparisons
were performed if each treatment group has at least 5 participants for yearly percentage
change from baseline in target PN volume after propensity score matching. In the model, the
yearly percentage change from baseline in target PN volume was the dependent variable, and
age and target PN volume at baseline were the covariates.
